Image name: FD-FR_task3_gt2_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 1
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are G (black) and I (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from I.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node G is connected to: X, K, F, H.
    *   Node I is connected to: E, H, B, C, J, P.

2.  **Analyze all possible paths from G to I:**
    All paths originating from G and leading towards I must first pass through one of G's direct neighbors: X, K, F, or H.
    *   Paths through X: G-X-F... (requires F)
    *   Paths through K: G-K-F... (requires F)
    *   Paths through F: G-F-E-I, G-F-E-H-I, G-F-E-B-I, G-F-E-B-C-I, G-F-E-B-C-O-P-I, G-F-E-B-C-N-O-P-I, G-F-E-L-M-J-I, G-F-E-M-J-I, etc.
    *   Paths through H: G-H-I, G-H-E-I, G-H-B-I, G-H-B-C-I, etc.

3.  **Identify critical edges for disconnection:**
    Notice that any path from G to I must necessarily traverse either the edge (G, F) or the edge (G, H).
    *   If a path starts G-X or G-K, it must then proceed to F (e.g., G-X-F or G-K-F) to continue towards I, as X and K are not directly connected to any other nodes leading to I. Thus, these paths also rely on F.
    *   Therefore, all paths from G to I must pass through either F or H.

4.  **Propose a cut set based on this observation:**
    To disconnect G from I, we need to sever all paths. Since all paths from G to I must use either F or H as the next step from G, removing the edges (G, F) and (G, H) would effectively isolate G from any route to I.

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   If edge (G, F) is removed, G can no longer reach F. This breaks all paths that start G-F, G-X-F, or G-K-F.
    *   If edge (G, H) is removed, G can no longer reach H. This breaks all paths that start G-H.
    *   With both (G, F) and (G, H) removed, G is only connected to X and K. Neither X nor K are connected to I or any node that can lead to I without passing through F or H (which are now disconnected from G).
    *   Therefore, removing (G, F) and (G, H) completely disconnects G from I.

6.  **Determine minimality:**
    *   Can we disconnect G from I by removing only one edge?
        *   If we remove only (G, F), paths like G-H-I still exist.
        *   If we remove only (G, H), paths like G-F-E-I still exist.
        *   Since removing a single edge is not sufficient, the minimum number of edges must be at least 2.
    *   Since we found a cut set of 2 edges that works, this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(G, F), (G, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': [['F', 'E'], ['G', 'H']]}

Image name: FD-FR_task2_gt2_PAIR3
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 1
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are M and B.
2.  **Check for direct connection:** There is no direct line connecting M and B. Therefore, disconnection is possible.
3.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 1:**
    *   Consider removing any single node directly connected to M: L, E, or J.
        *   If L is removed, paths like M-E-H-B or M-J-I-B still exist.
        *   If E is removed, paths like M-L-E-H-B (broken) but M-J-I-B still exists.
        *   If J is removed, paths like M-E-H-B still exist.
    *   Consider removing any single node directly connected to B: H, I, C, or N.
        *   If H is removed, paths like M-E-I-B or M-J-P-C-B still exist.
        *   If I is removed, paths like M-E-H-B or M-J-P-C-B still exist.
        *   If C is removed, paths like M-E-H-B or M-J-I-B still exist.
        *   If N is removed, paths like M-E-H-B or M-J-I-B still exist.
    *   Since removing any single node does not disconnect M from B, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

4.  **Explore paths and identify potential cut sets of size 2:**
    Let's list the main pathways from M to B:
    *   **Paths starting M-L-E...:**
        *   M-L-E-H-B
        *   M-L-E-I-B
    *   **Paths starting M-E... (not through L):**
        *   M-E-H-B
        *   M-E-I-B
        *   M-E-F-G-H-B
    *   **Paths starting M-J...:**
        *   M-J-I-B
        *   M-J-P-I-B
        *   M-J-P-C-B
        *   M-J-P-O-N-B
        *   M-J-D-P-I-B
        *   M-J-D-P-C-B
        *   M-J-D-P-O-N-B

    Notice that all paths from M to B must pass through one of M's direct neighbors: L, E, or J.
    *   If we remove node **E**:
        *   All paths starting with M-L-E... are broken.
        *   All paths starting with M-E... are broken.
        *   The only remaining way for M to reach the rest of the graph is through node J.
        *   After removing E, M is still connected to B via paths like M-J-I-B, M-J-P-C-B, etc.
    *   If we additionally remove node **J**:
        *   All paths starting with M-J... are broken.
        *   Since E was already removed, all paths starting with M-L-E... and M-E... are also broken.
        *   With both E and J removed, M has no remaining paths to B.

5.  **Verify the cut set [E, J]:**
    *   If E is removed: M can still reach B via J (e.g., M-J-I-B).
    *   If J is removed: M can still reach B via E (e.g., M-E-H-B).
    *   If both E and J are removed:
        *   All paths from M that go through E (M-L-E-..., M-E-...) are severed.
        *   All paths from M that go through J (M-J-...) are severed.
        *   Since all paths from M to B must start with L, E, or J, and L only connects to E (which is removed), M is completely disconnected from B.
    *   Therefore, [E, J] is a valid cut set.

6.  **Confirm minimality:** Since we established that a single node removal is not sufficient, and we found a cut set of size 2, this is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, J]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 2, 'cut_set': ['J', 'E']}

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are E and I. My goal is to disconnect them by removing the minimum number of white nodes.

2.  **Analyze Connections from E:**
    *   E is directly connected to H, G, and M.
    *   Any path from E to I must start by going through one of these three nodes.

3.  **Analyze Connections from I:**
    *   I is directly connected to H, J, P, and C.
    *   Any path from E to I must end by arriving at I from one of these four nodes.

4.  **Trace All Paths and Identify Bottlenecks:**
    *   **Path Group 1 (via H):**
        *   E - H - I: This is a direct path.
        *   E - G - F - H - I: Paths from E through G must pass through F, and then H, to reach I. (Nodes G, F, K, X form a sub-graph where F is the only connection to H, and H is the only connection to I from this sub-graph).
        *   Therefore, all paths from E that go through G eventually converge on H to reach I.
        *   **Conclusion for Group 1:** Node H is a critical bottleneck for all paths that pass through H or G. Removing H will sever all these paths.

    *   **Path Group 2 (via M):**
        *   E - M - J - I
        *   E - M - J - P - I
        *   E - M - J - D - P - I
        *   Node M is connected to J. Node J is connected to I, P, and D. Node L is connected to M but not to I or any of I's neighbors.
        *   **Conclusion for Group 2:** All paths from E that go through M must pass through J to reach I. Therefore, either M or J is a critical bottleneck for these paths.

5.  **Determine Minimum Cut Set:**
    *   To disconnect E and I, we must break both Path Group 1 and Path Group 2.
    *   To break Path Group 1, we must remove H.
    *   To break Path Group 2, we must remove either M or J.
    *   This means a minimum cut set will consist of H and one of {M, J}.
    *   Possible minimal cut sets of size 2 are [H, M] or [H, J].

6.  **Verify Single Node Removal:**
    *   If only H is removed, paths like E-M-J-I still exist.
    *   If only M is removed, paths like E-H-I still exist.
    *   If only J is removed, paths like E-H-I still exist.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

7.  **Final Check with Cut Set [H, J]:**
    *   If H is removed: All paths E - H - I and E - G - (F/K/X)* - H - I are broken.
    *   If J is removed: All paths E - M - J - I, E - M - J - P - I, and E - M - J - D - P - I are broken.
    *   After removing H and J:
        *   E is connected to G and M.
        *   I is connected to P and C.
        *   Can E-G reach P or C? G is connected to F, K, X. None of F, K, X are connected to P or C. No.
        *   Can E-M reach P or C? M is connected to L. L is not connected to P or C. No.
    *   Thus, E and I are completely disconnected.

The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, J]

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt2_PAIR2

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G (black) and N (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from N.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate neighbors:**
    *   Neighbors of G: X, K, F, E, H
    *   Neighbors of N: B, C, O, Y

2.  **Consider a simple approach: isolating one of the target nodes.**
    *   To isolate N, I would need to remove all edges connected to it. These are (N,B), (N,C), (N,O), and (N,Y). This would be 4 edges.
    *   However, node Y is only connected to N. It does not provide any alternative paths to G. Therefore, removing (N,Y) is not strictly necessary to disconnect N from G, as long as all other paths are cut.
    *   If I remove (N,B), (N,C), and (N,O), node N would only be connected to Y. Since Y is only connected to N, N would be effectively isolated from the rest of the graph, including G. This gives a cut set of 3 edges: [(N,B), (N,C), (N,O)].

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 1 or 2 edges) is possible.**
    *   To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to find at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and N. If I can find 3 such paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum cut size is at least 3.

4.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths from G to N:**
    *   **Path 1:** G - H - B - N
        *   Edges: (G,H), (H,B), (B,N)
    *   **Path 2:** G - E - M - J - P - O - N
        *   Edges: (G,E), (E,M), (M,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,N)
    *   **Path 3:** G - F - E - H - C - N
        *   Edges: (G,F), (F,E), (E,H), (H,C), (C,N)

5.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    *   **Path 1 vs Path 2:**
        *   (G,H) vs (G,E) - Different
        *   (H,B) vs (E,M) - Different
        *   (B,N) vs (M,J) - Different
        *   (J,P) - Only in P2
        *   (P,O) - Only in P2
        *   (O,N) - Only in P2
        *   No common edges. Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.
    *   **Path 1 vs Path 3:**
        *   (G,H) vs (G,F) - Different
        *   (H,B) vs (F,E) - Different
        *   (B,N) vs (E,H) - Different
        *   (H,C) - Only in P3
        *   (C,N) - Only in P3
        *   No common edges. Path 1 and Path 3 are edge-disjoint.
    *   **Path 2 vs Path 3:**
        *   (G,E) vs (G,F) - Different
        *   (E,M) vs (F,E) - Different
        *   (M,J) vs (E,H) - Different
        *   (J,P) vs (H,C) - Different
        *   (P,O) vs (C,N) - Different
        *   (O,N) - Only in P2
        *   No common edges. Path 2 and Path 3 are edge-disjoint.

6.  **Conclusion on minimum cut size:**
    *   Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and N, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.
    *   I previously identified a cut set of 3 edges: [(N,B), (N,C), (N,O)]. This set successfully disconnects N from the rest of the graph (and thus from G).
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, B), (N, C), (N, O)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are labeled I and E. I will perform a breadth-first search (BFS) starting from node I to find the shortest path to node E.

1.  **Initialize:**
    *   Queue: `[(I, [I])]` (Node, Path)
    *   Visited: `{}`

2.  **Level 0:**
    *   Dequeue `(I, [I])`. Current node is I.
    *   Neighbors of I: J, H, C, P.
    *   Enqueue `(J, [I, J])`, `(H, [I, H])`, `(C, [I, C])`, `(P, [I, P])`.
    *   Mark I, J, H, C, P as visited.
    *   Queue: `[(J, [I, J]), (H, [I, H]), (C, [I, C]), (P, [I, P])]`

3.  **Level 1:** (Paths of length 1 from I)
    *   Dequeue `(J, [I, J])`. Current node is J.
        *   Neighbors of J: D, P, M (I is visited).
        *   Enqueue `(D, [I, J, D])`, `(M, [I, J, M])`. (P is visited)
        *   Mark D, M as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(H, [I, H])`. Current node is H.
        *   Neighbors of H: C, X, G (I, C are visited).
        *   Enqueue `(X, [I, H, X])`, `(G, [I, H, G])`.
        *   Mark X, G as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(C, [I, C])`. Current node is C.
        *   Neighbors of C: N, B (I, H are visited).
        *   Enqueue `(N, [I, C, N])`, `(B, [I, C, B])`.
        *   Mark N, B as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(P, [I, P])`. Current node is P.
        *   Neighbors of P: D, O (I, J, D are visited).
        *   Enqueue `(O, [I, P, O])`.
        *   Mark O as visited.
    *   At this point, no path of length 1 or 2 has reached the target node E.
    *   Queue: `[(D, [I, J, D]), (M, [I, J, M]), (X, [I, H, X]), (G, [I, H, G]), (N, [I, C, N]), (B, [I, C, B]), (O, [I, P, O])]`

4.  **Level 2:** (Paths of length 2 from I)
    *   Dequeue `(D, [I, J, D])`. Neighbors of D (J, P) are all visited.
    *   Dequeue `(M, [I, J, M])`. Neighbors of M: L (J, O are visited).
        *   Enqueue `(L, [I, J, M, L])`.
        *   Mark L as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(X, [I, H, X])`. Neighbors of X: K (H, G are visited).
        *   Enqueue `(K, [I, H, X, K])`.
        *   Mark K as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(G, [I, H, G])`. Neighbors of G: F (H, X, K are visited).
        *   Enqueue `(F, [I, H, G, F])`.
        *   Mark F as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(N, [I, C, N])`. Neighbors of N: Y (O, B, C are visited).
        *   Enqueue `(Y, [I, C, N, Y])`.
        *   Mark Y as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(B, [I, C, B])`. Current node is B.
        *   Neighbors of B: N, C, E, F (N, C, F are visited).
        *   Check E: Is E the target node? YES!
        *   Path found: `[I, C, B, E]`.

The path `[I, C, B, E]` has a length of 3 edges. Since BFS explores layer by layer, the first path found to the target node is guaranteed to be the shortest.

**Verification:**
*   Is I connected to C? Yes.
*   Is C connected to B? Yes.
*   Is B connected to E? Yes.
All connections are valid.

Image name: POGBa_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 1
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H (black) and N (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and N.

1.  **Identify direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node H is connected to: G, I, B.
    *   Node N is connected to: C, B, O.

2.  **Consider a simple cut strategy:** One straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all edges directly connected to it.
    *   If I remove all edges connected to N: (N, C), (N, B), (N, O). This would isolate N from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from H. This cut set has 3 edges.
    *   If I remove all edges connected to H: (H, G), (H, I), (H, B). This would isolate H from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from N. This cut set also has 3 edges.

3.  **Explore for a smaller cut (e.g., 2 edges):** To determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 2 edges) is possible, I need to check if there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N. If there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum edge cut must be at least 3.

    Let's try to find three edge-disjoint paths from H to N:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges: (H, B), (B, N)

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        Edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)

    *   **Path 3:** H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O - N
        Edges: (H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    *   Edges in Path 1: (H, B), (B, N)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)

    Upon inspection, all edges listed in these three paths are unique to their respective paths. There are no shared edges between any pair of these three paths. Therefore, these are three edge-disjoint paths from H to N.

5.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:** Since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3. As we already identified a cut set of 3 edges (e.g., [(N, C), (N, B), (N, O)]), this is indeed the minimum.

6.  **Final verification of the chosen cut set:** If we remove the edges (N, C), (N, B), and (N, O), node N becomes isolated from all other nodes in the graph. Consequently, no path can exist between H and N.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, C), (N, B), (N, O)]

Image name: PGS_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 1
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are H and N.
2.  **Goal:** Determine the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H from N.
3.  **Strategy:** I will use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes. I will first try to find a set of edge-disjoint paths, and then identify a corresponding cut set.

4.  **Identify paths from H to N:**
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        *   Edges: (H, B), (B, N)
    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        *   Edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   **Path 3:** H - G - E - B - O - N
        *   Edges: (H, G), (G, E), (E, B), (B, O), (O, N)

5.  **Check for edge-disjointness:**
    *   Path 1 uses edges: (H, B), (B, N)
    *   Path 2 uses edges: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   Path 3 uses edges: (H, G), (G, E), (E, B), (B, O), (O, N)

    Upon inspection, there are no common edges between these three paths. For example, (H, B) is only in Path 1, (H, I) is only in Path 2, and (H, G) is only in Path 3. Similarly, the edges connecting to N are distinct: (B, N) in Path 1, (C, N) in Path 2, and (O, N) in Path 3. Even though Path 3 uses node B, it does so via edges (E, B) and (B, O), which are distinct from (H, B) and (B, N) used in Path 1. Therefore, these three paths are edge-disjoint.

6.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and N, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

7.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    *   Consider the edges directly connected to node H: (H, G), (H, B), and (H, I).
    *   If these three edges are removed, node H becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
    *   If H is isolated, no path can exist between H and N.
    *   This cut set, [(H, G), (H, B), (H, I)], consists of 3 edges.

8.  **Conclusion:** Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and a cut set of 3 edges, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, G), (H, B), (H, I)]

    *   M is connected to E, J, and L.
    *   If we remove all edges incident to M: (E, M), (J, M), (L, M), M would be completely isolated. This is a cut of size 3.

3.  **Check for a cut of size 1:**
    *   If we remove (E, M), paths like F-G-H-I-J-M still exist.
    *   If we remove (J, M), paths like F-E-M still exist.
    *   If we remove (L, M), paths like F-E-M and F-G-H-I-J-M still exist.
    *   If we remove (F, E), paths like F-G-H-E-M and F-G-H-I-J-M still exist.
    *   If we remove (G, H), paths like F-E-M still exist.
    *   No single edge removal can disconnect F and M. Therefore, the minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

4.  **Check for a cut of size 2:**
    *   Let's identify critical "bottleneck" edges. All paths from F to M either go directly through E (via F-E) or pass through G and then H (via G-H).
    *   **Option 1: Cut (E, M) and (G, H).**
        *   Removing (E, M) breaks the direct path F-E-M and any path that uses E to reach M (e.g., F-G-H-E-M).
        *   After removing (E, M), any remaining paths from F to M must use J to reach M. These paths all originate from F, pass through G, then H, then I (or B, C, N, O, Y, P) to eventually reach J, and then M.
        *   Removing (G, H) breaks all paths that pass through G and H. This includes all remaining paths from F to M (e.g., F-G-H-I-J-M, F-K-G-H-I-J-M, etc.).
        *   Therefore, the set of edges [(E, M), (G, H)] successfully disconnects F from M. This cut has a size of 2.

    *   **Option 2: Cut (F, E) and (G, H).**
        *   Removing (F, E) breaks the direct path F-E-M.
        *   After removing (F, E), any remaining paths from F to M must pass through G (e.g., F-G-H-E-M, F-G-H-I-J-M).
        *   Removing (G, H) breaks all paths that pass through G and H. This includes all remaining paths from F to M.
        *   Therefore, the set of edges [(F, E), (G, H)] also successfully disconnects F from M. This cut has a size of 2.

        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (no common intermediate nodes).

According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect J and E is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut set must contain at least 3 nodes.

4.  **Identify a Candidate Cut Set:** Based on the structure of these disjoint paths, the nodes M, F, and B appear to be critical "bottlenecks" for these three distinct routes to E.

Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 1
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E and H.

First, I identify all edges directly connected to H, as any path to H must use one of these edges.
The edges connected to H are:
1. (H, E) - This is the direct connection to the other target node.
2. (H, G)
3. (H, B)
4. (H, I)

If I remove all these four edges, H will become an isolated node, meaning no path can exist between E and H, or any other node for that matter. This constitutes a cut set of size 4: [(E,H), (G,H), (B,H), (I,H)]. This proves that the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 4.

Now, I need to determine if it's possible to disconnect E and H by removing fewer than 4 edges (i.e., 3 or fewer). To do this, I will attempt to find 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If I can find 4 edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum edge cut is 4.

Let's list edge-disjoint paths from E to H:

1.  **Path 1:** E - H
    *   Edges used: {(E, H)}

2.  **Path 2:** E - G - H
    *   Edges used: {(E, G), (G, H)}
    *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

3.  **Path 3:** E - M - J - I - H
    *   Edges used: {(E, M), (M, J), (J, I), (I, H)}
    *   This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

4.  **Path 4:** E - L - M - J - P - O - N - B - H
    *   Let's check if this path is edge-disjoint from the previous three.
    *   Edges used in Path 4: {(E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N), (N, B), (B, H)}
    *   Comparing with Path 3: Path 3 uses (M, J). Path 4 also uses (M, J).
    *   Therefore, Path 4 as defined is NOT edge-disjoint from Path 3.

My attempt to find 4 edge-disjoint paths failed with the above selection. Let's try to find a fourth path that is truly edge-disjoint from the first three.

Edges used by P1, P2, P3:
P1: (E,H)
P2: (E,G), (G,H)
P3: (E,M), (M,J), (J,I), (I,H)

Remaining unused edges from E: (E,F), (E,L)
Remaining unused edges from H: (H,B)

Let's try to construct a fourth path using (E,F) or (E,L) and ending with (H,B).

*   **Attempt with (E,F):**
    *   E - F - G... (G is used by P2, specifically (E,G) and (G,H))
    *   E - F - K... (K is connected to F, G, X. G is used.)
    *   E - F - K - X... (X is connected to F, G, K. G is used.)
    *   It seems difficult to reach B from F without using edges already in P1, P2, or P3, especially (G,H).

*   **Attempt with (E,L):**
    *   E - L - M... (M is used by P3, specifically (E,M) and (M,J))
    *   Since (M,J) is used, any path through M to J is not edge-disjoint from P3. L is only connected to E and M. So, E-L-M cannot be used to reach B without using (M,J).

This systematic search suggests that there are only 3 edge-disjoint paths. If there are only 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is 3.

Let's re-evaluate my previous conclusion that 3 edges are not enough.
If the minimum cut is 3, then there must be a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects E and H.
Let's consider the set of edges that are "entry points" to H: (E,H), (G,H), (B,H), (I,H).
If we remove any 3 of these 4 edges, one edge will remain. For example, if we remove [(E,H), (G,H), (I,H)], the edge (B,H) remains.
Is there a path from E to B that does not use (E,H), (G,H), or (I,H)?
Yes: E - M - J - P - O - N - B.
Since (B,H) remains, the path E - M - J - P - O - N - B - H still exists.
Therefore, removing [(E,H), (G,H), (I,H)] is NOT sufficient to disconnect E and H.

This confirms that 3 edges are not enough. Since 4 edges (all incident to H) are sufficient, and 3 edges are not, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

The cut set consists of all edges directly connected to H.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(E, H), (G, H), (B, H), (I, H)]

Image name: POGBa_task1_gt4_PAIR1
Task: task1_shortest_path_length
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 1
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are labeled L and Y. I will perform a breadth-first search (BFS) starting from node L to find the shortest path to node Y.

1.  **Initialize:**
    *   Queue: `[(L, [L])]` (stores tuples of (current_node, path_to_current_node))
    *   Visited: `{L}` (stores nodes for which the shortest path has been found)

2.  **Level 0:**
    *   Dequeue `(L, [L])`.
    *   Current node is L.

3.  **Level 1:** Neighbors of L
    *   L is connected to M and E.
    *   Enqueue `(M, [L, M])`. Add M to Visited.
    *   Enqueue `(E, [L, E])`. Add E to Visited.
    *   Queue: `[(M, [L, M]), (E, [L, E])]`
    *   Visited: `{L, M, E}`

4.  **Level 2:** Neighbors of nodes from Level 1

    *   Node Y is only connected to N. It does not lead to H or any node connected to H. Therefore, any path starting with (N, Y) is a dead end for reaching H.
    *   This means all paths from N to H must pass through either O, C, or B.

3.  **Identify potential "bottleneck" edges:**
    To disconnect N from H, we need to ensure that no path exists between them. A straightforward approach is to cut all outgoing edges from N that lead to H, or all incoming edges to H that originate from N.

    Let's consider cutting the edges directly connected to N, excluding the dead-end (N, Y):
    *   Edge (N, O)
    *   Edge (N, C)
    *   Edge (N, B)

4.  **Form a candidate cut set:**
    Let's propose the cut set S = [(N, O), (N, C), (N, B)]. This set contains 3 edges.

5.  **Verify the candidate cut set:**
    If these three edges are removed:
    *   N is no longer connected to O.
    *   N is no longer connected to C.
    *   N is no longer connected to B.
    Since N is only connected to Y (which is a dead end for H) after removing these edges, N becomes completely disconnected from the rest of the graph, and thus from H. All paths from N to H are broken.

6.  **Determine if this is the minimum number of edges:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we need to show that there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and H. If we can find 3 paths that do not share any common edges, then by Menger's theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect N from H.

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** N - C - H
        *   Edges: (N, C), (C, H)
    *   **Path 2:** N - B - H
        *   Edges: (N, B), (B, H)
    *   **Path 3:** N - O - P - I - H
        *   Edges: (N, O), (O, P), (P, I), (I, H)

    By inspecting these three paths, we can see that all the edges listed are distinct. For example, (N, C) is distinct from (N, B) and (N, O). (C, H) is distinct from (B, H) and (I, H). And so on for all edges.
    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect N from H must be at least 3.
    As our candidate cut set has a size of 3, it is indeed the minimum.

    *   If we remove 2 edges, say (M, O) and (M, J), a path like M-L-E-B still exists.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from M to B:

    Upon inspection, these three paths do not share any common edges. They are completely edge-disjoint.

6.  **Conclusion from Menger's Theorem:**
    Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths from M to B, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect M from B is at least 3.

    *   **Node L:** L is connected to M and E. If node E is removed, L becomes isolated from the rest of the graph (except M).     *   **Node E:** E is connected to B, L, M, and P. Paths through E include B -> E -> M and B -> E -> L -> M.
    *   **Node J:** J is connected to M, P, and D. Paths through J include B -> ... -> P -> J -> M.

    *   Therefore, the two critical "bottleneck" nodes that all paths from B to M must traverse are E and J.

        *   All paths that go through E (including those that would go through L) are broken.
        *   All paths that go through J are broken.

5.  **Verify the cut set:**
    The cut set is [(L, M), (L, E)].
    *   All paths from L to J must use either (L, M) or (L, E) as their first edge.
    *   Removing (L, M) breaks all paths starting L-M.
    *   Removing (L, E) breaks all paths starting L-E.
    *   Therefore, removing both edges breaks all possible paths from L to J.

Step-by-step reasoning:

1.  **Identify the direct neighbors of H and E:**
    *   Neighbors of H: G, I, B
    *   Neighbors of E: M, F, B, L

2.  **Consider a simple approach: removing all edges connected to one of the target nodes.**
    *   To disconnect H, I can remove all edges connected to it: (H, G), (H, I), (H, B). This is a cut set of size 3. After removing these edges, H is isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from E.
    *   To disconnect E, I can remove all edges connected to it: (E, M), (E, F), (E, B), (E, L). This is a cut set of size 4.

    Since a cut set of size 3 has been found, the minimum number of edges to remove is at most 3.

3.  **Attempt to find edge-disjoint paths between H and E to establish a lower bound for the cut size (Menger's Theorem).** If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then at least *k* edges must be removed.
    Let's try to find three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - E
        Edges: {(H, B), (B, E)}
    *   **Path 2:** H - G - F - E
        Edges: {(H, G), (G, F), (F, E)}
    *   **Path 3:** H - I - P - J - M - E
        Edges: {(H, I), (I, P), (P, J), (J, M), (M, E)}

    Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
    *   Edges in Path 1: (H, B), (B, E)
    *   Edges in Path 2: (H, G), (G, F), (F, E)
    *   Edges in Path 3: (H, I), (I, P), (P, J), (J, M), (M, E)

    Upon inspection, there are no common edges between any pair of these three paths. Therefore, these are three edge-disjoint paths from H to E.

4.  **Determine the minimum cut size.**
    According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect H and E is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between H and E. Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size must be at least 3.
    As we already identified a cut set of size 3 (by removing all edges connected to H: [(H, G), (H, I), (H, B)]), this set must be a minimal cut.

5.  **Final verification of the chosen cut set:**
    *   Removing (H, G) breaks all paths that start with H-G.
    *   Removing (H, I) breaks all paths that start with H-I.
    *   Removing (H, B) breaks all paths that start with H-B.
    Since all paths from H to E must begin with one of these three edges, removing them ensures that no path exists between H and E.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, G), (H, I), (H, B)]

4.  Now, let's consider removing two nodes. Based on our analysis of D's connections:
    *   To cut all paths to J, we must remove I.
    *   To cut all paths to P, we must remove I AND O (because P is connected to both I and O).
    *   Therefore, if we remove both I and O:
        *   Removing I severs all connections to J (since J is only connected to I).
        *   Removing I also severs all connections to P that pass through I.
        *   Removing O severs the remaining connections to P that pass through O (specifically, the path E -> B -> C -> N -> O -> P -> D).
    *   With both I and O removed, no path exists from E to D.
5.  Since we have found a set of 2 nodes ([I, O]) that disconnects E from D, and we've determined that 1 node is not sufficient, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [I, O]

Step-by-step reasoning. The two black nodes are labeled X and N. I will perform a breadth-first search (BFS) starting from node X to find the shortest path to node N.

1.  **Initialize:**
    *   Queue: `[(X, [X])]` (Node, Path)
    *   Visited: `{X}`

2.  **Level 1:**
    *   Dequeue `(X, [X])`.
    *   Neighbors of X: K, G, F.
    *   Add K to queue: `(K, [X, K])`. Mark K as visited.
    *   Add G to queue: `(G, [X, G])`. Mark G as visited.
    *   Add F to queue: `(F, [X, F])`. Mark F as visited.
    *   Queue: `[(K, [X, K]), (G, [X, G]), (F, [X, F])]`
    *   Visited: `{X, K, G, F}`

3.  **Level 2:**
    *   Dequeue `(K, [X, K])`. Neighbors of K: X (visited), F (visited), G (visited). No new unvisited neighbors.
    *   Dequeue `(G, [X, G])`. Neighbors of G: X (visited), K (visited), F (visited), H.
        *   Add H to queue: `(H, [X, G, H])`. Mark H as visited.
    *   Dequeue `(F, [X, F])`. Neighbors of F: X (visited), K (visited), G (visited), E.
        *   Add E to queue: `(E, [X, F, E])`. Mark E as visited.
    *   Queue: `[(H, [X, G, H]), (E, [X, F, E])]`
    *   Visited: `{X, K, G, F, H, E}`

4.  **Level 3:**
    *   Dequeue `(H, [X, G, H])`. Neighbors of H: G (visited), B, I, E (visited).
        *   Add B to queue: `(B, [X, G, H, B])`. Mark B as visited.
        *   Add I to queue: `(I, [X, G, H, I])`. Mark I as visited.

        *   Add M to queue: `(M, [X, F, E, M])`. Mark M as visited.
    *   Queue: `[(B, [X, G, H, B]), (I, [X, G, H, I]), (L, [X, F, E, L]), (M, [X, F, E, M])]`
    *   Visited: `{X, K, G, F, H, E, B, I, L, M}`

        *   Node N is the target node!
        *   The path found is `[X, G, H, B, N]`.
        *   The length of this path is 4 (number of edges).

*   Is G directly connected to H? Yes.
*   Is H directly connected to B? Yes.
*   Is B directly connected to N? Yes.

All connections are valid.

        *   Can L, M, or F reach C, J, or P?

            *   M is connected to E, L, F.
            *   F is connected to E, M, X, G, K.
        None of L, M, F have direct or indirect connections to C, J, or P without going through B. For example, C is connected to B, N, I, O. N is connected to B, C, Y, O. O is connected to Y, N, C, P. J is connected to D, I, P. P is connected to D, J, I, O. All these nodes (C, J, P, N, O, D, Y) are part of a sub-graph that is only connected to E via B.
        Therefore, any path from E to I must necessarily pass through B.

4.  **Formulate a potential cut set:**
    Based on the analysis in step 3, if we remove the edge (E, B), we effectively disconnect E from all paths that would go through B or I to reach H. This is because B is the only gateway from E to the sub-graph containing B, N, C, Y, O, P, J, D, and I.

    After removing (E, B), the only remaining way for E to reach H is through G.

5.  **Analyze paths through G:**
    *   E - F - G - H
    *   E - F - X - G - H
    *   E - M - F - G - H
    *   E - M - F - X - G - H
    *   E - L - M - F - G - H
    *   E - L - M - F - X - G - H

    All these paths eventually reach G. To disconnect H from G, we must remove the edge (H, G).

6.  **Proposed Cut Set:**
    The proposed cut set is [(E, B), (H, G)]. This set has 2 edges.

7.  **Verify the proposed cut set:**
    *   Removing (E, B) breaks all paths from E to H that pass through B or I.
    *   Removing (H, G) breaks all paths from E to H that pass through G.
    *   Since all paths from E to H must pass through B, G, or I, removing these two edges ensures complete disconnection.

8.  **Check for minimality:**
    *   Can we disconnect E and H by removing only one edge?
        *   If we remove (E, B), paths like E-F-G-H still exist.
        *   If we remove (H, G), paths like E-B-H still exist.
        *   If we remove any other single edge, there will always be at least one path remaining.
    *   Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.
    *   Since we found a cut set of size 2 that successfully disconnects E and H, this is the minimum.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, B), (H, G)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [[['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I'], ['E', 'F']], [['H', 'B'], ['H', 'I'], ['H', 'G']]]}

    Both of these paths rely on the connection between E and M to proceed towards J. If the edge (E, M) is removed, both Path 1 and Path 2 are broken. Alternatively, removing (M, J) would also break these paths. For this reasoning, I will choose (E, M).

    *   Paths starting with F-X: F - X - G - ... - J and F - X - K - G - ... - J.
    *   Paths starting with F-K: F - K - G - ... - J and F - K - X - G - ... - J.
    Notice that all paths from F that go through X or K eventually converge at node G. From node G, the only outgoing edge is (G, H). Therefore, removing the edge (G, H) will effectively cut off all paths from F that pass through X or K, regardless of their specific route to G.

5.  **Formulate a potential cut set:**

        *   If we remove (E, M), paths through X and K (e.g., F-X-G-H-...) still exist.
        *   If we remove (G, H), paths through E (e.g., F-E-M-J) still exist.
        *   If we remove any single edge directly connected to F (e.g., (F, E)), other paths (e.g., F-X-G-H-...) still exist.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E (black) and J (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E and J.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node E is connected to: F, G, H, L, M.
    *   Node J is connected to: M, I, P, D.

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    A straightforward approach to disconnect two nodes is to isolate one of them by removing all its incident edges.
    *   To isolate E, we would need to remove 5 edges: (E, F), (E, G), (E, H), (E, L), (E, M).
    *   To isolate J, we would need to remove 4 edges: (M, J), (I, J), (P, J), (D, J).
    Removing 4 edges is a smaller number than 5. So, a cut set of size 4 is possible: `[(M, J), (I, J), (P, J), (D, J)]`. If these 4 edges are removed, J becomes an isolated node, and thus no path can exist between E and J.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 3 edges) is possible:**
    To prove that 4 is the minimum, we need to show that no set of 3 edges can disconnect E and J.
    Let's consider all possible paths from E to J. All paths from E to J must eventually pass through one of J's direct neighbors (M, I, P, D) and then use the edge connecting that neighbor to J.

    Let's test if removing any 3 edges from the set `{(M, J), (I, J), (P, J), (D, J)}` is sufficient to disconnect E and J.
    *   **Scenario 1: Remove (M, J), (I, J), (P, J).**
        The edge (D, J) remains. We need to check if there's a path from E to D.
        Consider the path: E - H - I - P - D - J.
        The edges in this path are `(E, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, D), (D, J)`.
        None of these edges are in the removed set `{(M, J), (I, J), (P, J)}`.
        Therefore, this path `E - H - I - P - D - J` is still open, and E is still connected to J. This set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

    *   **Scenario 2: Remove (M, J), (I, J), (D, J).**
        The edge (P, J) remains. We need to check if there's a path from E to P.
        Consider the path: E - H - I - P - J.
        The edges in this path are `(E, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, J)`.
        None of these edges are in the removed set `{(M, J), (I, J), (D, J)}`.
        Therefore, this path `E - H - I - P - J` is still open, and E is still connected to J. This set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

    *   **Scenario 3: Remove (M, J), (P, J), (D, J).**
        The edge (I, J) remains. We need to check if there's a path from E to I.
        Consider the path: E - H - I - J.
        The edges in this path are `(E, H), (H, I), (I, J)`.
        None of these edges are in the removed set `{(M, J), (P, J), (D, J)}`.
        Therefore, this path `E - H - I - J` is still open, and E is still connected to J. This set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

    *   **Scenario 4: Remove (I, J), (P, J), (D, J).**
        The edge (M, J) remains. We need to check if there's a path from E to M.
        Consider the path: E - M - J.
        The edges in this path are `(E, M), (M, J)`.
        None of these edges are in the removed set `{(I, J), (P, J), (D, J)}`.
        Therefore, this path `E - M - J` is still open, and E is still connected to J. This set of 3 edges is not sufficient.

    Since removing any 3 of the 4 edges directly connected to J is not sufficient to disconnect E and J, and these 4 edges represent all direct connections to J, it implies that any cut set must contain at least 4 edges.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    A cut set of 4 edges `[(M, J), (I, J), (P, J), (D, J)]` successfully disconnects E and J. We have demonstrated that no set of 3 edges can achieve this disconnection. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

The final answer is:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(M, J), (I, J), (P, J), (D, J)]

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (e.g., 2 edges) is possible.**

    Let's identify three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path 1:** H - I - C - B
        Edges: `(H, I)`, `(I, C)`, `(C, B)`

    *   Edges in Path 1: `(H, I)`, `(I, C)`, `(C, B)`
    *   Edges in Path 2: `(H, X)`, `(X, F)`, `(F, E)`, `(E, B)`

    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B, Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of edges required to disconnect H and B is at least 3.
    As I previously identified a cut set of size 3 (by removing all edges connected to H: `[(H, I), (H, G), (H, X)]`), and this cut set successfully disconnects H from B, this is indeed the minimum number of edges.

6.  **Confirm minimality using Menger's Theorem (optional but good for verification):**

        Edges: (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, P), (P, D)

Neighbors of E: P, L, M, B

1.  **Path through P:**
    *   I - P - E
    *   Other paths that use P as a bridge:
        *   I - J - P - E
        *   I - C - O - P - E
        *   I - C - N - O - P - E
        *   I - H - G - F - P - E
    If node P is removed, all these paths are broken.

2.  **Path through C and B:**
    *   I - C - B - E
    This path does not use P. If C or B is removed, this path is broken.

3.  **Path through J and M:**
    *   I - J - M - E
    This path does not use P, C, or B. If J or M is removed, this path is broken.

Now, let's identify vertex-disjoint paths between I and E. Vertex-disjoint paths share no common intermediate nodes.

*   **Path 1:** I - P - E
    *   Intermediate node: {P}

*   **Path 2:** I - C - B - E
    *   Intermediate nodes: {C, B}
    This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 because C and B are not P.

*   **Path 3:** I - J - M - E
    *   Intermediate nodes: {J, M}
    This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 (J, M are not P) and Path 2 (J, M are not C, B).

Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between I and E, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect I and E is 3.

To form a cut set, I must select one node from each of these disjoint paths (excluding I and E).
*   From Path 1: P (P is the only intermediate node)
*   From Path 2: C or B
*   From Path 3: J or M

Therefore, a minimal cut set will always include P, and then one node from {C, B} and one node from {J, M}.

Let's choose one such cut set, for example, [P, C, J].
If P, C, and J are removed:
*   The path I - P - E is broken because P is removed.
*   The path I - C - B - E is broken because C is removed.
*   The path I - J - M - E is broken because J is removed.
*   All other paths identified (e.g., I - J - P - E, I - C - O - P - E, I - H - G - F - P - E) are also broken because they contain P, C, or J.

Thus, removing these 3 nodes successfully disconnects I and E. No smaller set of nodes can achieve this because we found 3 vertex-disjoint paths.

    *   H - B - N (uses edge (B, N))
    *   H - B - C - N (uses edge (B, N) and (C, N))

2.  **Paths through I:**
    *   H - I - C - N (uses edge (C, N))
    *   H - I - P - O - N (uses edge (O, N))

4.  **Paths through G:**
    *   H - G - E - M - J - P - O - N (uses edge (O, N))

To disconnect H from N, we must ensure that no path exists between them. This means we need to cut all paths.
A common strategy is to identify a "cut set" of edges that, when removed, separates the graph into two components, with H in one and N in the other.

Let's consider the edges directly connected to N, excluding (N, Y) as it's a dead-end for paths from H:
*   (B, N)
*   (C, N)
*   (O, N)

Let's examine if removing these three edges disconnects H from N:
1.  **Remove (B, N):** This cuts the direct path H - B - N. It also cuts the path H - B - C - N at its connection to N.
2.  **Remove (C, N):** This cuts the path H - I - C - N.
3.  **Remove (O, N):** This cuts the path H - I - P - O - N. It also cuts the paths H - E - M - J - P - O - N and H - G - E - M - J - P - O - N.

Now, I need to verify if this is the *minimum* number of edges.
I can identify three edge-disjoint paths from H to N, meaning they do not share any common edges.
*   **Path 1:** H - B - N (uses edges (H, B) and (B, N))
*   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N (uses edges (H, I), (I, C), and (C, N))
*   **Path 3:** H - E - M - J - P - O - N (uses edges (H, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), and (O, N))

These three paths are indeed edge-disjoint. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Since I found three edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut must be at least 3.

Since I found a cut set of size 3, and the minimum required is at least 3, this is the minimal cut.

4.  **Determine if a smaller cut (size 2) is possible using Menger's Theorem:**

    Let's try to find 3 edge-disjoint paths from E to J:

    *   **Path 1:** E - F - K - J
        Edges: `{(E,F), (F,K), (K,J)}`

    *   **Path 2:** E - B - C - I - J

        Edges in Path 1: (E,F), (F,K), (K,J)
        Edges in Path 2: (E,B), (B,C), (C,I), (I,J)
        These two paths do not share any common edges, so they are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3:** Now we need to find a third path from E to J that does not use any of the edges from Path 1 or Path 2.

- It's clear that removing a single edge will not disconnect M and B, as there are multiple distinct paths between them. For example, if I remove (M, E), paths through J (e.g., M-J-I-H-B) and through L (M-L-E-B) still exist.

**3. Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**
I will try to identify two edges whose removal would break all paths.
Let's categorize the main "entry points" from M to the rest of the graph, and "exit points" from the graph to B.

**Main branches from M:**
*   **Branch 1 (via E):** Paths starting M-E... or M-L-E...
    *   M - E - B
    *   M - L - E - B
    *   M - E - F - G - H - B (and variations through K, X)
    *   M - L - E - F - G - H - B (and variations through K, X)
*   **Branch 2 (via J):** Paths starting M-J...
    *   M - J - I - H - B
    *   M - J - I - C - B
    *   M - J - P - I - H - B
    *   M - J - P - O - C - B
    *   M - J - P - O - N - B

Let's test combinations of 2 edges:
*   **If I remove (M, E) and (M, J):**
    *   Paths M-E-B and M-E-F-G-H-B are cut.
    *   Paths M-J-I-H-B, M-J-I-C-B, M-J-P-I-H-B, M-J-P-O-C-B, M-J-P-O-N-B are cut.
    *   However, the path **M - L - E - B** (and M-L-E-F-G-H-B) still exists because (M, L) is not cut, and (L, E) and (E, B) are not cut. So, [(M, E), (M, J)] is not enough.
*   **If I remove (M, E) and (L, E):**
    *   Paths M-E-B, M-E-F-G-H-B, M-L-E-B, M-L-E-F-G-H-B are cut.
    *   However, all paths starting with M-J... are still active. So, [(M, E), (L, E)] is not enough.
*   **If I remove (E, B) and (H, B):**
    *   Paths M-E-B, M-L-E-B, M-E-F-G-H-B, M-L-E-F-G-H-B are cut by (E, B).
    *   Paths M-J-I-H-B and M-J-P-I-H-B are cut by (H, B).
    *   However, paths that reach B via C or N are still active:
        *   M - J - I - C - B
        *   M - J - P - O - C - B
        *   M - J - P - O - N - B
    *   So, [(E, B), (H, B)] is not enough.

Based on these attempts, it appears that 2 edges are insufficient to disconnect M and B. This suggests the minimum cut is 3.

**4. Confirm a cut set of size 3:**
I already identified one cut set of size 3: [(M, E), (M, L), (M, J)], which isolates M.

Let's try to find another cut set of size 3 that cuts the "flow" of paths more strategically.
Consider the three main "channels" through which M can reach B:
1.  **Through E:** This channel includes paths like M-E-B and M-L-E-B. The edge (E, B) is critical for these.
2.  **Through J-I:** This channel includes paths like M-J-I-H-B and M-J-I-C-B. The edge (J, I) is critical for these.
3.  **Through J-P:** This channel includes paths like M-J-P-I-H-B, M-J-P-O-C-B, and M-J-P-O-N-B. The edge (J, P) is critical for these.

Let's propose the cut set: **[(E, B), (J, I), (J, P)]**.
*   **Removing (E, B):** This breaks all paths that go directly from E to B. This includes:
    *   M - E - B
    *   M - L - E - B
    *   M - E - F - G - H - B (and variations through K, X)
    *   M - L - E - F - G - H - B (and variations through K, X)
*   **Removing (J, I):** This breaks all paths that go from J to I. This includes:
    *   M - J - I - H - B
    *   M - J - I - C - B
*   **Removing (J, P):** This breaks all paths that go from J to P. This includes:
    *   M - J - P - I - H - B (as it uses J-P)
    *   M - J - P - O - C - B (as it uses J-P)
    *   M - J - P - O - N - B (as it uses J-P)

By removing these three edges, all identified paths from M to B are broken. Since I've systematically shown that 2 edges are not sufficient, and I've found a valid cut set of 3 edges, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, B), (J, I), (J, P)]

Image name: FD-FR_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 1
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My task is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H and B. This is a minimum edge cut problem. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

First, I will try to identify a set of edge-disjoint paths between H and B. If I can find N edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size must be at least N.

Let's identify potential edge-disjoint paths:
1.  **Path 1 (P1): H - B**
    *   Edges: {(H, B)}

2.  **Path 2 (P2): H - E - B**
    *   Edges: {(H, E), (E, B)}
    *   This path is edge-disjoint from P1.

3.  **Path 3 (P3): H - I - C - B**
    *   Edges: {(H, I), (I, C), (C, B)}
    *   This path is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2 (no common edges).

4.  **Path 4 (P4): H - F - E - M - J - P - I - B**
    *   Edges: {(H, F), (F, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, I), (I, B)}
    *   Let's check if P4 is edge-disjoint from P1, P2, and P3:
        *   P1: (H, B) - No common edges with P4.
        *   P2: (H, E), (E, B) - No common edges with P4.
        *   P3: (H, I), (I, C), (C, B) - No common edges with P4.
    *   Therefore, P4 is edge-disjoint from P1, P2, and P3.

I have successfully identified 4 edge-disjoint paths between H and B.
P1: H - B
P2: H - E - B
P3: H - I - C - B
P4: H - F - E - M - J - P - I - B

Since there are 4 edge-disjoint paths, by Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and B is at least 4.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 4 edges to confirm that 4 is indeed the minimum.
A common strategy for finding a minimal cut is to identify a partition of the graph's nodes into two sets, one containing H and the other containing B, such that the number of edges crossing this partition is minimized.

Let's define a set of nodes S containing H, and a set T containing B, such that S ∪ T = V (all nodes) and S ∩ T = ∅.
Let S = {H, G, F, K, X}. These are H and its neighbors that are not directly connected to B or B's immediate vicinity (E, I, C, N).
Let T = {B, E, I, C, N, O, Y, P, J, D, M, L}. These are B and all other remaining nodes.

Now, I list all edges that connect a node in S to a node in T:
1.  (H, B): H ∈ S, B ∈ T
2.  (H, E): H ∈ S, E ∈ T
3.  (H, I): H ∈ S, I ∈ T
4.  (F, E): F ∈ S, E ∈ T

These are all the edges that cross the boundary between S and T. If these edges are removed, there will be no path from H (in S) to B (in T).
The cut set is **[(H, B), (H, E), (H, I), (F, E)]**.
The size of this cut set is 4.

Since I found 4 edge-disjoint paths (proving the minimum is at least 4) and I found a cut set of 4 edges (proving the minimum is at most 4), the minimum number of edges to remove is exactly 4.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, E), (H, I), (F, E)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['B', 'H'], ['H', 'I'], ['G', 'H']]}

            *   B -> N -> C -> I -> J
            *   B -> N -> O -> P -> J
            *   B -> C -> I -> J
            *   B -> C -> O -> P -> J
        *   All these paths from E to J go through B.

5.  **Identify Critical Nodes (Cut Set):**
    *   Based on the path analysis, any path from E to J must pass through either node F (to access the K/G/X part of the graph) or node B (to access the H/N/C/I/P part of the graph).
    *   Therefore, removing both F and B will sever all possible connections between E and J. This forms a cut set: {F, B}.

6.  **Verify Minimum Number of Nodes:**
    *   **Can it be done with 1 node?**
        *   If I remove F, paths through B remain (e.g., E -> B -> H -> I -> J).
        *   If I remove B, paths through F remain (e.g., E -> F -> K -> J).
        *   If I remove any other single node (e.g., K, I, P, H, G, C, N, O, X, L, M, D, Y), there will always be at least one path through F and at least one path through B that remains active. For example, removing K breaks paths like E-F-K-J, but E-B-H-I-J remains. Removing I breaks paths like E-B-H-I-J, but E-F-K-J remains.
    *   Since no single node removal can disconnect E and J, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

7.  **Conclusion:** The cut set {F, B} has a size of 2, and we have established that a single node removal is insufficient. Therefore, 2 is the minimum number of nodes.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [F, B]

4.  **Search for vertex-disjoint paths:** To determine the minimum cut size, I will look for vertex-disjoint paths between E and I. By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes in a vertex cut is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between the two nodes.
    Let's try to find three vertex-disjoint paths (excluding E and I):
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - C - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C}
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - G - H - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {F, G, H}
    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - P - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J, P}

    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {F, G, H}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {M, J, P}.
    These three sets are mutually disjoint. Therefore, there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between E and I. This implies that the minimum number of nodes to remove (the minimum cut set size) must be at least 3.

    Since the minimum cut size is at least 3, I will now look for a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects E and I.
    Consider the direct neighbors of E: B, F, M, L.
    Any path from E to I must start by traversing one of these edges.
    *   If a path starts E -> B, it must pass through B.
    *   If a path starts E -> F, it must pass through F.
    *   If a path starts E -> M, it must pass through M.

    Therefore, if I remove nodes B, F, and M, all initial connections from E to the rest of the graph (that could lead to I) are severed.
    Let's verify the cut set {B, F, M}:
    *   Removing B breaks all paths that start E -> B.
    *   Removing F breaks all paths that start E -> F.
    *   Removing M breaks all paths that start E -> M.
    *   Since L is only connected to M, removing M also isolates L from E's potential paths to I.
    After removing B, F, and M, node E is only connected to L, and L is no longer connected to any path leading to I. Thus, E is completely disconnected from I.

6.  **Conclusion:** I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, which means the minimum cut size is at least 3. I have also found a cut set of size 3 ({B, F, M}) that successfully disconnects E and I. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

    *   Consider removing any single node. M has neighbors L, E, J. N has neighbors B, C, O, Y. There are clearly multiple paths between M and N that do not share a single intermediate node. For example, M-E-...-N and M-J-...-N are distinct starting branches. Therefore, a single node removal is insufficient. The minimum cut set size must be at least 2.

3.  **Identify Node-Disjoint Paths (Menger's Theorem):** To confirm the minimum cut size, I will look for the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between M and N (excluding M and N themselves).
    *   **Path 1:** M - E - H - B - N. The intermediate nodes are {E, H, B}.
    *   **Path 2:** Now, I look for a path from M to N that does not use any of {E, H, B}.
        *   From M, I can go to L or J.
        *   If M -> L, then L connects to E. But E is already used in Path 1. So M-L-E is not node-disjoint from Path 1.
        *   If M -> J, J is not in {E, H, B}.
            *   From J, I can go to I or P.
            *   Let's try J -> I. I is not in {E, H, B}.
                *   From I, I can go to H (used), C, J (used), P.
                *   Let's try I -> C. C is not in {E, H, B}.
                    *   From C, I can go to B (used), I (used), O, N.
                    *   Let's try C -> N.
                *   So, **Path 2:** M - J - I - C - N. The intermediate nodes are {J, I, C}.
    *   **Check for Node-Disjointness:** Path 1 ({E, H, B}) and Path 2 ({J, I, C}) are completely node-disjoint.

    *   **Search for a Third Node-Disjoint Path:** Now, I need to find a path from M to N that does not use any of the nodes from Path 1 or Path 2: {E, H, B, J, I, C}.
        *   From M, the remaining neighbor is L. L is not in the used set.
            *   M -> L. From L, the only connection is to E (and M). E is in the used set. So, M-L-E is blocked.
        *   All other paths from M start with E or J, which are in the used set.
    *   Since I can only find two node-disjoint paths, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect M and N is 2.

4.  **Identify a Minimum Cut Set (Size 2):**
    *   Based on the node-disjoint paths, a cut set of size 2 should exist. I will look for two nodes whose removal severs all paths.
    *   Consider removing nodes **E** and **J**.
        *   If **E** is removed: All paths from M that start with M-E are broken. This includes paths like M-E-H-B-N, M-E-H-C-N, M-E-G-H-B-N, etc.
        *   If **J** is removed: All paths from M that start with M-J are broken. This includes paths like M-J-I-C-N, M-J-I-H-B-N, M-J-P-O-N, etc.
        *   After removing E and J, M is only connected to L. L is connected to M and E. Since E is removed, L cannot connect M to any other part of the graph. Therefore, M is completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from N.
    *   This confirms that {E, J} is a valid cut set of size 2.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from J. I cannot remove E or J.

First, I will systematically identify all possible paths from E to J.
From E, there are three main "entry points" to the rest of the graph that can lead to J: M, F, and B.

Let's analyze the paths based on these entry points:

**Paths starting E -> M or E -> F:**
These paths eventually lead to K, X, G, and H.
From H, the only direct connection towards J's side of the graph is to I.
So, any path from E that goes through M or F must eventually pass through H, and then through I, to reach J.
Examples:
*   E - M - K - X - G - H - I - J
*   E - M - K - F - G - H - I - J
*   E - F - K - X - G - H - I - J
*   E - F - K - M - E (cycle, not to J)

**Paths starting E -> B:**
From B, paths can go to H, C, or N.
*   **Via H:** E - B - H - I - J (This path uses I)
*   **Via C:**
    *   E - B - C - I - J (This path uses I)
    *   E - B - C - N - O - P - J (This path uses P)
    *   E - B - C - N - O - Y - J (This path uses Y)
*   **Via N:**
    *   E - B - N - O - P - J (This path uses P)
    *   E - B - N - O - Y - J (This path uses Y)

Now, let's consider the nodes that are directly connected to J. These are I, P, D, and Y.
Node D is a leaf node connected only to J and P. If P is removed, D is only connected to J. Thus, D cannot be an intermediate node on a path from E to J if P is removed. If P is not removed, then any path through D would also pass through P. So, D is not a critical node for disconnection.

Therefore, all paths from E to J must pass through I, P, or Y.

Let's try to find a minimum cut set.

**Can a single node disconnect E and J?**
Let's test by removing any single node (not E or J):
*   If I is removed: Paths like E - B - N - O - P - J still exist.
*   If P is removed: Paths like E - B - C - I - J still exist.
*   If Y is removed: Paths like E - B - C - I - J and E - B - C - N - O - P - J still exist.
*   If B is removed: Paths like E - M - K - X - G - H - I - J still exist.
*   If H is removed: Paths like E - B - C - I - J and E - B - N - O - P - J still exist.
*   If C is removed: Paths like E - B - H - I - J and E - B - N - O - P - J still exist.
*   If O is removed: Paths like E - B - C - I - J and E - B - H - I - J still exist.
*   If N is removed: Paths like E - B - C - I - J and E - B - H - I - J still exist.
*   If M is removed: Paths like E - B - C - I - J and E - F - K - X - G - H - I - J still exist.
*   If F is removed: Paths like E - B - C - I - J and E - M - K - X - G - H - I - J still exist.
*   If K is removed: Paths like E - B - C - I - J and E - M - L - E (cycle) and E - F - E (cycle) still exist.
*   If X is removed: Paths like E - B - C - I - J and E - M - K - F - G - H - I - J still exist.
*   If G is removed: Paths like E - B - C - I - J and E - B - H - I - J still exist.
*   Removing any other node (L, D) will not disconnect E from J as they are either leaf nodes or part of cycles not leading to J.

Since removing any single node does not disconnect E from J, the minimum cut set must be of size 2 or more.

**Can two nodes disconnect E and J?**
Let's consider the critical nodes that all paths must pass through.
*   **Observation 1:** Any path from E that goes through M or F must eventually pass through H, and then through I, to reach J. (e.g., E - M - K - X - G - H - I - J). So, all paths not using B must use I.
*   **Observation 2:** Any path from E that goes through B can either go through H (and then I), or through C (and then I, P, or Y), or through N (and then O, P, or Y).
    *   If a path from B uses I (e.g., E - B - H - I - J or E - B - C - I - J), it uses I.
    *   If a path from B uses P (e.g., E - B - C - N - O - P - J or E - B - N - O - P - J), it uses B.
    *   If a path from B uses Y (e.g., E - B - C - N - O - Y - J or E - B - N - O - Y - J), it uses B.

Let's rephrase:
*   All paths from E to J that *do not* use node B must use node I (because they go through M/F -> K/X/G -> H -> I -> J).
*   All paths from E to J that *do not* use node I must use node B (because they go through B -> C/N -> O -> P/Y -> J).

This means that every single path from E to J must pass through either node B or node I.
Therefore, if we remove both B and I, all paths between E and J will be broken.

Let's verify this cut set [B, I]:
1.  If B is removed: All paths starting E -> B are severed. E can now only reach M, L, F. From M or F, the only way to reach J is through K, X, G, H, and then I.
2.  If I is also removed: All paths that went through H to I (from M/F/K/X/G) are now broken. Also, any paths that went through C to I are broken.
Since all paths from E must pass through either B or I, removing both B and I will disconnect E from J.

This is a cut set of size 2. Since we've established that a cut set of size 1 is not possible, this is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [B, I]

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node N is connected to: Y, O, B.
    *   Node H is connected to: B, G, I.

2.  **Consider all paths from N to H:**
    Any path from N to H must eventually use one of the edges directly connected to H, or one of the edges directly connected to N.

    Let's list some representative paths to understand the structure:
    *   **Direct path:** N - B - H
    *   **Paths through B (from N):**
        *   N - B - H
        *   N - B - E - F - X - G - H
        *   N - B - E - F - K - G - H
    *   **Paths through O (from N):**
        *   N - O - C - B - H
        *   N - O - C - I - H
        *   N - O - P - I - H
        *   N - O - P - J - M - L - E - F - X - G - H
        *   N - O - P - J - M - L - E - F - K - G - H
    *   **Paths through Y (from N):**
        *   N - Y - O - C - B - H
        *   N - Y - O - C - I - H
        *   N - Y - O - P - I - H
        *   N - Y - O - P - J - M - L - E - F - X - G - H
        *   N - Y - O - P - J - M - L - E - F - K - G - H

3.  **Determine the minimum number of edges to cut:**
    To disconnect H from N, all paths leading to H must be broken. Since H is only connected to nodes B, G, and I, any path reaching H must use one of the edges (B, H), (G, H), or (I, H).

    *   If we remove only one edge (e.g., (B, H)), paths through G and I would still exist (e.g., N - O - C - I - H, N - B - E - F - X - G - H).
    *   If we remove any two edges (e.g., (B, H) and (G, H)), paths through the remaining edge (I, H) would still exist (e.g., N - O - C - I - H).
    *   Therefore, to ensure no path exists to H, all three edges connected to H must be removed. These edges are (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).

4.  **Verify the cut set:**

3.  **Propose a cut set:** If we remove nodes B, G, and I, then H will have no remaining neighbors, and thus no path can exist from E to H. This forms a cut set of size 3: [B, G, I].
4.  **Verify minimality using vertex-disjoint paths:** To confirm that 3 is the minimum number of nodes, we look for vertex-disjoint paths between E and H (paths that share no intermediate nodes).
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H
        *   Intermediate node: {B}
    *   **Path 2:** E - F - K - G - H
        *   Intermediate nodes: {F, K, G}

    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - D - P - I - H
        *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J, D, P, I}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2 (no common intermediate nodes).
5.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Since we have found three vertex-disjoint paths between E and H, Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect E and H (excluding E and H themselves) must be at least 3.
6.  **Conclusion:** We found a cut set of size 3 ([B, G, I]) and determined that at least 3 nodes are required. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.
7.  **Verify necessity of each node in the cut set:**
    *   If we remove only B and G, the path E - M - J - D - P - I - H still exists.
    *   If we remove only B and I, the path E - F - K - G - H still exists.
    *   If we remove only G and I, the path E - B - H still exists.

Image name: Tu_task3_gt2_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 1
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between F and J.

1.  **Identify direct connections of F and J:**
    *   F is connected to: P, G, K, X.
    *   J is connected to: P, I, M, D.

2.  **Check for a cut of size 1:**
    I will test if removing any single edge can disconnect F and J.
    *   If I remove (F, P), paths like F-G-H-I-J still exist.
    *   If I remove (F, G), paths like F-P-J still exist.
    *   If I remove (G, H), paths like F-P-J still exist.
    *   If I remove (I, J), paths like F-P-J still exist.
    *   If I remove (P, J), paths like F-G-H-I-J still exist.
    Since multiple distinct paths exist between F and J, removing a single edge is not sufficient. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.

3.  **Explore potential cut sets of size 2:**
    I need to find two edges whose removal breaks all paths between F and J.
    Let's analyze the structure of the graph around F. F has four direct connections: (F, P), (F, G), (F, K), (F, X).
    *   **Consider paths starting with (F, P):** Any path from F that begins with the edge (F, P) will be broken if (F, P) is removed. Examples: F-P-J, F-P-E-M-J, F-P-D-J.
    *   **Consider paths starting with (F, G), (F, K), or (F, X):** These three edges connect F to a local cluster of nodes: {F, G, K, X}. Within this cluster, nodes are connected (e.g., (G, K), (G, X), (K, X)). To reach J from F via any of these three edges, the path must eventually leave this {F, G, K, X} cluster. By inspecting the graph, the only edge that connects this {F, G, K, X} cluster to the rest of the graph (excluding the direct connection to P from F, which is already considered) is (G, H).
        *   Therefore, if a path from F does *not* use (F, P), it *must* use (G, H) to proceed towards J. Examples: F-G-H-I-J, F-K-G-H-B-E-M-J, F-X-G-H-I-J.

4.  **Propose a cut set:**
    Based on the observation above, if I remove:
    *   Edge (F, P): This breaks all paths that start from F through P.
    *   Edge (G, H): This breaks all paths that start from F through G, K, or X, as (G, H) is the only "exit" from the {F, G, K, X} cluster to the rest of the graph (excluding the P branch).

5.  **Verify the proposed cut set: [(F, P), (G, H)]**
    *   **Path type 1 (via P):** Any path from F to J that uses node P (e.g., F-P-J, F-P-E-M-J, F-P-D-J) is broken by the removal of (F, P).
    *   **Path type 2 (via G, K, or X):** Any path from F to J that uses nodes G, K, or X (e.g., F-G-H-I-J, F-K-G-H-B-E-M-J, F-X-G-H-I-J) must pass through the edge (G, H) to leave the {F, G, K, X} cluster and reach J. The removal of (G, H) breaks all such paths.
    Since all possible paths from F to J fall into one of these two categories, removing both (F, P) and (G, H) ensures that no path exists between F and J.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    The cut set [(F, P), (G, H)] successfully disconnects F and J. Since we determined that 1 edge is not sufficient, and we found a cut set of 2 edges, this is the minimum number of edges required.

The final answer is $\boxed{\text{Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, P), (G, H)]}}$

2.  **Identify all possible paths between H and N:** I will systematically list paths, looking for distinct routes.
    *   **Path 1 (via B):** H - B - N
    *   **Path 2 (via I and C):** H - I - C - N
    *   **Path 3 (via G, K, J, P, O):** H - G - K - J - P - O - N

3.  **Check for vertex-disjoint paths:** I will examine the intermediate nodes (nodes other than H and N) in the identified paths to see if they share any nodes.
    *   Intermediate nodes in Path 1: {B}
    *   Intermediate nodes in Path 2: {I, C}
    *   Intermediate nodes in Path 3: {G, K, J, P, O}
    The sets of intermediate nodes {B}, {I, C}, and {G, K, J, P, O} are all distinct. This means these three paths are vertex-disjoint.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Menger's theorem states that the minimum number of vertices that must be removed to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices (s and t) is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between s and t. Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and N, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects H from N.
    Consider the direct neighbors of H: G, B, I. If all these nodes are removed, H will have no connections to any other white node in the graph, thus disconnecting it from N.
    Let's verify this cut set: [G, B, I].
    *   Removing B breaks Path 1 (H-B-N) and any other paths that use B (e.g., H-B-C-N, H-B-O-N).
    *   Removing I breaks Path 2 (H-I-C-N) and any other paths that use I (e.g., H-I-P-O-N, H-G-...-I-C-N).
    *   Removing G breaks Path 3 (H-G-K-J-P-O-N) and any other paths that use G (e.g., H-G-F-...).
    Since H is only connected to G, B, and I, removing these three nodes will isolate H from the rest of the graph, including N. Therefore, [G, B, I] is a valid cut set of size 3.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, and I found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [G, B, I]

    No single node removal disconnects E and I. Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

4.  **Identify Node-Disjoint Paths (Menger's Theorem):** To determine the minimum cut size, I can look for node-disjoint paths between E and I. If I find *k* node-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut size is at least *k*.
    Let's try to find three node-disjoint paths (excluding E and I):
    *   **Path 1:** E - G - I (Intermediate node: G)
    *   **Path 2:** E - M - J - I (Intermediate nodes: M, J)
    *   **Path 3:** E - B - H - I (Intermediate nodes: B, H)

    Let's check if the sets of intermediate nodes are disjoint:
    *   {G}
    *   {M, J}
    *   {B, H}
    These three sets are indeed disjoint. This means there are at least 3 node-disjoint paths between E and I. Therefore, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3.

5.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:** Since the minimum is at least 3, I will try to find a cut set of size 3.
    Consider the nodes that are the "first step" from E into the main branches leading to I:
    *   Node G (for paths like E-G-I, E-F-G-I)
    *   Node M (for paths like E-M-J-I, E-L-M-J-I)
    *   Node B (for paths like E-B-H-I, E-B-C-I)

    Let's propose the cut set: **[M, G, B]**
    *   If **M** is removed: All paths starting with E-M... (E-M-J-I, E-L-M-J-I) are broken.
    *   If **G** is removed: All paths starting with E-G... (E-G-I, E-F-G-I, E-F-K-G-I, E-F-X-K-G-I) are broken.
    *   If **B** is removed: All paths starting with E-B... (E-B-H-I, E-B-C-I, E-B-N-C-I, E-B-N-Y-O-C-I, E-B-C-O-P-I, E-B-N-Y-O-P-I) are broken.

    *   F is connected to X, K, and G. G is removed. X and K are connected to G (removed) and F (which is connected to E). So, F cannot reach I.
    Therefore, removing M, G, and B successfully disconnects E from I.
