The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, F and J. I will systematically identify all possible paths between F and J and then determine a minimal set of edges whose removal breaks all these paths.

First, I list all direct connections (edges) in the graph, paying close attention to the visual representation to ensure accuracy:
Nodes: F (black), J (black), K, X, G, H, I, P, D, M, E, B, C, O, Y, L, N

Edges:
(F, K), (F, X), (F, G), (F, E)
(J, P), (J, I), (J, D), (J, M)
(K, X), (K, G)
(X, G)
(G, H)
(H, I), (H, B)
(I, C), (I, P)
(P, D), (P, O)
(E, M), (E, B), (E, L)
(M, L)
(B, C), (B, N)
(C, N), (C, O)
(O, Y), (O, N)

Next, I identify the main "channels" or "branches" through which paths can travel from F to J. All paths from F must start by traversing one of F's direct edges: (F, K), (F, X), (F, G), or (F, E). Similarly, all paths to J must end by traversing one of J's direct edges: (J, P), (J, I), (J, D), or (J, M).

I can categorize the paths into three main independent groups based on their initial divergence from F:

1.  **Paths through G**: These paths start from F, go through G, and then proceed towards J.
    *   Example path: F -> G -> H -> I -> J
    *   Other paths through G would involve F -> G -> H -> B -> ... -> J.
    *   The edge (G, H) acts as a critical bridge for all paths originating from F and passing through G to reach the right side of the graph (towards J). Removing (G, H) would cut off all such paths.

2.  **Paths through E and then M**: These paths start from F, go through E, then M, and finally to J.
    *   Example path: F -> E -> M -> J
    *   Another path: F -> E -> L -> M -> J
    *   The edge (M, J) is the final connection to J for all paths that pass through M. Removing (M, J) would cut off all such paths. Note that cutting (E, M) and (L, M) would also disconnect M from E and L, but this would require two edges, making it less efficient than cutting (M, J) if (M, J) is the only edge connecting M to J.

3.  **Paths through E and then B**: These paths start from F, go through E, then B, and then proceed towards J.
    *   Example path: F -> E -> B -> H -> I -> J
    *   Other paths through B would involve F -> E -> B -> C -> ... -> J or F -> E -> B -> N -> ... -> J.
    *   The edge (E, B) acts as a critical bridge for all paths originating from F and passing through E to reach B and subsequently the right side of the graph (towards J). Removing (E, B) would cut off all such paths.

Now, I propose a cut set consisting of one edge from each of these three main channels:
Cut Set: **[(G, H), (M, J), (E, B)]**

Let's verify if removing these three edges completely disconnects F from J:
*   Removing (G, H) breaks all paths that go from F through G to H and beyond (e.g., F-G-H-I-J, F-G-H-B-C-I-J, etc.).
*   Removing (M, J) breaks all paths that go from F through E (or E-L) to M and then to J (e.g., F-E-M-J, F-E-L-M-J).
*   Removing (E, B) breaks all paths that go from F through E to B and beyond (e.g., F-E-B-H-I-J, F-E-B-C-I-J, F-E-B-N-O-P-J, etc.).

Since all paths from F to J must pass through either G, or E-M, or E-B, and we have cut an essential edge in each of these three independent channels, F and J are completely disconnected.

Finally, I need to confirm that this is the *minimum* number of edges. I will test if removing any two of these edges would still leave a path:
1.  If (G, H) and (M, J) are removed: The path F -> E -> B -> H -> I -> J (or any other path through E-B) still exists.
2.  If (G, H) and (E, B) are removed: The path F -> E -> M -> J (or F -> E -> L -> M -> J) still exists.
3.  If (M, J) and (E, B) are removed: The path F -> G -> H -> I -> J (or any other path through G-H) still exists.

Since removing any two edges from the proposed cut set leaves at least one path between F and J, all three edges are necessary. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(G, H), (M, J), (E, B)]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, J and E. This is a minimum (s,t)-cut problem, which by Menger's theorem is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between J and E.

First, I will identify all possible paths between J and E. Then, I will attempt to find a set of edge-disjoint paths. Finally, I will propose a cut set and verify its effectiveness and minimality.

**1. Identify Paths from J to E:**
Let's list the direct neighbors of J: M, D, P, I.
Let's list the direct neighbors of E: L, M, F, B.

Paths can be broadly categorized by their first edge from J and their last edge to E.

*   **Paths starting J-M:**
    *   J - M - E
    *   J - M - L - E

*   **Paths starting J-D:**
    *   J - D - P - O - N - B - E
    *   J - D - P - O - C - B - E
    *   J - D - P - O - C - I - H - B - E
    *   J - D - P - O - C - I - H - G - K - X - F - E

*   **Paths starting J-P (not through D):**
    *   J - P - O - N - B - E
    *   J - P - O - C - B - E
    *   J - P - O - C - I - H - B - E
    *   J - P - O - C - I - H - G - K - X - F - E

*   **Paths starting J-I:**
    *   J - I - C - B - E
    *   J - I - C - O - N - B - E
    *   J - I - H - B - E
    *   J - I - H - G - K - X - F - E

**2. Find Edge-Disjoint Paths:**
To determine the minimum cut, I will look for edge-disjoint paths. If I find `k` edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is at least `k`.

Let's try to find three edge-disjoint paths:
*   **Path 1:** J - M - E
    *   Edges: (J,M), (M,E)

*   **Path 2:** J - P - O - N - B - E
    *   Edges: (J,P), (P,O), (O,N), (N,B), (B,E)

*   **Path 3:** J - I - H - G - K - X - F - E
    *   Edges: (J,I), (I,H), (H,G), (G,K), (K,X), (X,F), (F,E)

Let's verify if these three paths are edge-disjoint:
*   The starting edges from J are (J,M), (J,P), and (J,I), which are distinct.
*   The ending edges to E are (M,E), (B,E), and (F,E), which are distinct.
*   All intermediate edges in these three paths are also distinct and do not overlap. For example, Path 2 uses nodes P, O, N, B, while Path 3 uses nodes I, H, G, K, X, F. There are no shared edges.

Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 3.

**3. Propose and Verify a Cut Set of Size 3:**
Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects J from E.

Consider the following cut set: **[(J,M), (B,E), (F,E)]**

Let's verify if removing these three edges breaks all paths from J to E:
*   **Removing (J,M):** This edge directly cuts all paths that start with J-M. This includes J-M-E and J-M-L-E. Thus, J can no longer reach M or L, and consequently cannot reach E via M or L.
*   **Removing (B,E):** This edge cuts all paths that reach E via node B. This includes all paths like J-D-P-O-N-B-E, J-D-P-O-C-B-E, J-D-P-O-C-I-H-B-E, J-P-O-N-B-E, J-P-O-C-B-E, J-P-O-C-I-H-B-E, J-I-C-B-E, J-I-C-O-N-B-E, and J-I-H-B-E.
*   **Removing (F,E):** This edge cuts all paths that reach E via node F. This includes all paths like J-D-P-O-C-I-H-G-K-X-F-E, J-P-O-C-I-H-G-K-X-F-E, and J-I-H-G-K-X-F-E.

**Group 1: Paths through M**
*   E - M - J
*   E - L - M - J
All paths in this group require node M.

**Group 2: Paths through I**
*   E - I - J
*   E - I - P - J

*   E - H - B - C - O - P - J
*   E - F - G - H - B - N - O - P - J

1.  **If M is removed**: All paths in Group 1 are broken. However, paths in Group 2 (e.g., E-I-J) and Group 3 (e.g., E-H-B-N-O-P-J) still exist. So, removing M alone is not enough.
2.  **If I is removed**: All paths in Group 2 are broken. However, paths in Group 1 (e.g., E-M-J) and Group 3 (e.g., E-H-B-N-O-P-J) still exist. So, removing I alone is not enough.

    *   Paths in Group 1 (through M) are broken.
    *   Paths in Group 2 (through I) are broken.

    *   Paths in Group 1 (through M) are broken.
    *   Paths in Group 3 (through P) are broken.

        *   Therefore, 2 edges are not enough.

4.  **Verify the cut set of 3 edges:**

1.  O - I - H (direct connection)
2.  O - M - J - I - H
3.  O - P - D - J - I - H

1.  O - P - C - H
2.  O - M - J - D - P - C - H
3.  O - Y - N - B - C - H
4.  O - N - B - C - H
All paths in this group pass through node C. Removing C would break all of them.

**Group 3: Paths through nodes X or G**

-   Paths to X:
    *   O - Y - N - B - E - F - X - H
    *   O - Y - N - B - E - F - K - X - H

    *   O - N - B - E - F - G - H
    *   O - M - L - E - F - G - H
Notice that all paths in this group (to X or G) must pass through node F. Removing F would break all of them.

-   Node I (for Group 1 paths)
-   Node C (for Group 2 paths)
-   Node F (for Group 3 paths, which then lead to X or G)

    *   Path 4: B - H - G - F - M

    *   Path 6: B - H - G - K - F - M

    *   Removing the edge (F, M) will break all paths that go through F to reach M (Path 3, Path 4, Path 5, and Path 6).

    *   Therefore, removing a single edge is not sufficient to disconnect B and M.

    *   **Path 1:** I - J - M - E. The intermediate nodes are J and M.
    *   **Path 2:** I - H - G - F - E. The intermediate nodes are H, G, and F.
    *   **Path 3:** I - C - B - E. The intermediate nodes are C and B.

3.  **Verify Disjointness:**
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {J, M}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {H, G, F}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {C, B}.
    These three sets are mutually exclusive (disjoint). Therefore, these three paths are vertex-disjoint (except for the start and end nodes I and E).

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them. Since I have found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

5.  **Propose a Cut Set of Size 3:** Based on the disjoint paths, I will select one node from each path to form a cut set. A natural choice is to pick the nodes closest to E from each of these paths: M (from Path 1), F (from Path 2), and B (from Path 3). So, the proposed cut set is [M, F, B].

6.  **Verify the Cut Set [M, F, B]:**
    *   **Removing M:** This breaks Path 1 (I-J-M-E) and any other path that uses M (e.g., I-O-P-D-J-M-E).
    *   **Removing F:** This breaks Path 2 (I-H-G-F-E) and any other path that uses F (e.g., I-H-X-K-F-E, I-H-X-G-F-E).
    *   **Removing B:** This breaks Path 3 (I-C-B-E) and any other path that uses B (e.g., I-C-O-N-B-E, I-O-N-B-E, I-J-D-P-O-N-B-E, I-J-D-P-O-C-B-E, I-J-D-P-O-Y-N-B-E).

    After removing M, F, and B, the node E is only connected to L. There are no paths from I to L. Therefore, all paths from I to E are successfully disconnected.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, and we found a set of 3 nodes ([M, F, B]) that successfully disconnects I and E, this is the minimum number of nodes.

    Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient to disconnect G and I. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are L and J. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between L and J.

1.  **Identify direct connections of target nodes:**
    *   Node L is connected to M and E.
    *   Node J is connected to M, D, P, and I.

2.  **Consider paths starting from L:**
    Any path from L to J must begin with either the edge (L, M) or the edge (L, E).

3.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If I remove (L, M), paths like L-E-M-J or L-E-B-...-J still exist.
    *   If I remove (L, E), paths like L-M-J still exist.
    *   If I remove (M, J), paths like L-E-B-...-J or L-E-F-...-J (which eventually connect to J via D, P, or I) still exist.
    *   If I remove any single edge connected to J (e.g., (J, D), (J, P), or (J, I)), multiple other paths to J would still exist.
    Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient to disconnect L and J. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

4.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**

    *   **Option A: Isolate node L.**
        If I remove both edges connected to L, namely (L, M) and (L, E), then L becomes an isolated node. Since L has no outgoing connections, no path can originate from L and reach J.
        This cut set is [(L, M), (L, E)]. Its size is 2.

    *   **Option B: Cut critical paths at different points.**
        Let's consider the two main "entry points" from L to the rest of the graph: M and E.
        *   All paths from L to J either go through M directly (L-M-J) or through E.
        *   If a path goes through E, it can then go to M (L-E-M-J), or to B (L-E-B-...), or to F (L-E-F-...).

        Consider removing the edge (M, J). This immediately breaks the direct path L-M-J and the path L-E-M-J.
        After removing (M, J), any remaining path from L to J must start with L-E and then proceed through B or F to reach J (via D, P, or I).
        To cut all these remaining paths, I can remove the edge (L, E).
        If (L, E) is removed, L can only reach M. But since (M, J) was also removed, M cannot reach J. Therefore, L cannot reach J.
        This cut set is [(M, J), (L, E)]. Its size is 2.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Both Option A and Option B provide a valid cut set of size 2. Since we've established that a cut of size 1 is not possible, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

I will provide one of the valid cut sets.

The final answer is based on the reasoning that removing the two edges directly connected to node L, (L, M) and (L, E), will completely isolate L from the rest of the graph, thus ensuring no path exists between L and J.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are B and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between B and H.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to H:**
    Node H is directly connected to B, G, and I. This means any path from B to H must eventually use one of the edges (B, H), (G, H), or (I, H) as its final step to reach H.

2.  **Consider a cut set by isolating H:**
    If we remove all edges directly connected to H, H will become an isolated node, and thus no path can exist from B to H. The edges connected to H are (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H). Removing these three edges would disconnect H from the rest of the graph, including B. This gives a cut set of size 3: `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]`.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut set is possible (e.g., 1 or 2 edges):**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I will look for edge-disjoint paths between B and H. If I can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, at least *k* edges must be removed to disconnect the two nodes.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from B to H:
    *   **Path 1:** B - H
        Edges used: `{(B, H)}`
    *   **Path 2:** B - E - F - G - H
        Edges used: `{(B, E), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}`. This path does not use any edges from Path 1.
    *   **Path 3:** B - I - H
        Edges used: `{(B, I), (I, H)}`. This path does not use any edges from Path 1 or Path 2.

    Since I have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and H, according to Menger's Theorem, a minimum of 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    I found a cut set of size 3 `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]` that successfully disconnects B and H. I also proved that at least 3 edges are required by finding 3 edge-disjoint paths. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   This means all paths from E to J must pass through M, P, or I.

    *   **Can 1 node disconnect E and J?**

    *   This means the subgraph {G, X, K} becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
    *   Since N is not part of {G, X, K}, removing H and F successfully disconnects G from N.

5.  **Determine the minimum number of nodes:**
    *   Since a single node removal (H or F) is not enough, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.
    *   I have found a set of 2 nodes ([H, F]) that successfully disconnects G from N.
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, F]

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that, when removed, will completely disconnect G from N.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their immediate connections:**
    *   Node G (black) is connected to X, K, F, H.
    *   Node N (black) is connected to B, C, O, Y.

2.  **List all possible paths from G to N:**
    To systematically find a cut set, I'll consider the various routes from G to N. All paths from G must first pass through one of its neighbors (X, K, F, H). However, X and K are part of a local cluster with G and F, and paths from X or K to N would typically go through F or H. So, the primary "exit points" from G's immediate vicinity are H and F.

    Let's trace paths from G towards N:
    *   **Paths starting G-H-...**
        *   G-H-B-N
        *   G-H-B-C-N
        *   G-H-I-C-N
        *   G-H-I-P-O-N
        *   G-H-I-J-P-O-N
        *   G-H-I-J-D-P-O-N
    *   **Paths starting G-F-E-...** (Note: F is connected to G, and E is a key node for paths from F)
        *   G-F-E-H-... (These paths would then follow the G-H paths listed above)
        *   G-F-E-I-C-N
        *   G-F-E-I-P-O-N
        *   G-F-E-I-J-P-O-N
        *   G-F-E-I-J-D-P-O-N
        *   G-F-E-M-J-P-O-N
        *   G-F-E-M-J-D-P-O-N
        *   G-F-E-L-M-J-P-O-N
        *   G-F-E-L-M-J-D-P-O-N

3.  **Consider potential cut sets and their sizes:**

    *   **Cut all edges connected to N:** Removing (B,N), (C,N), (O,N), (Y,N) would isolate N. This is 4 edges. This is a valid cut, but we need to find the *minimum*.

    *   **Try to find a cut of 2 edges:**
        It's generally difficult to disconnect two well-connected parts of a graph with only two edges. Let's try some combinations:
        *   If we cut (H,B) and (E,I): Paths like G-H-I-C-N or G-F-E-M-J-P-O-N would still exist.
        *   If we cut (B,N) and (C,N): Paths like G-...-O-N or G-...-Y-N would still exist.
        *   Given the density of connections, a cut of 2 edges seems unlikely.

    *   **Try to find a cut of 3 edges:**
        Let's look for "bottleneck" edges that are crucial for multiple paths. I'll consider edges that bridge the gap between the "G-side" of the graph and the "N-side".
        Consider the edges: `(B, H)`, `(C, I)`, and `(O, P)`.
        Let's test this cut set: `Cut Set: [(B, H), (C, I), (O, P)]`

        *   **Check paths to N via B:**
            *   Any path from G to N that passes through B must use the edge (B,H) to reach B from G's side (e.g., G-H-B-N, G-F-E-H-B-N).
            *   Removing (B,H) breaks all such paths.

        *   **Check paths to N via C:**
            *   Paths to C from G can come via B (e.g., G-H-B-C-N) or via I (e.g., G-H-I-C-N, G-F-E-I-C-N).
            *   Removing (B,H) breaks paths via B.
            *   Removing (C,I) breaks paths via I.
            *   Therefore, all paths from G to C are broken.

        *   **Check paths to N via O:**
            *   Paths to O from G can come via P (e.g., G-H-I-P-O-N, G-F-E-I-P-O-N, G-F-E-M-J-P-O-N, G-F-E-L-M-J-P-O-N) or via C (e.g., G-H-I-C-O-N).
            *   Removing (O,P) breaks all paths that go through P to O.
            *   Removing (C,I) (and (B,H)) breaks all paths that go through C to O.
            *   Therefore, all paths from G to O are broken.

        *   **Check paths to N via Y:**
            *   Node Y is only connected to N and O.
            *   Since all paths from G to O are broken (as established above), there is no way for G to reach Y via O.
            *   Thus, N is completely disconnected from G.

4.  **Verify minimality:**
    To confirm that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that no set of 2 edges can disconnect G and N.
    *   If we remove (B,H) and (C,I): A path like G-H-I-P-O-N still exists (as (O,P) is not cut).
    *   If we remove (B,H) and (O,P): A path like G-H-I-C-N still exists (as (C,I) is not cut).
    *   If we remove (C,I) and (O,P): A path like G-H-B-N still exists (as (B,H) is not cut).
    Since removing any two edges from our proposed cut set leaves at least one path intact, the cut set of 3 edges is indeed minimal.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 3.
